1. Prior-art paragraph template for a SAT paper

You could include something like this:

The SAT construction intersects several established mathematical traditions. Its use of curve-based degrees of freedom is related to worldline and proper-time formulations of quantum field theory, as well as Frenet–Serret and rigid-particle actions involving curvature and torsion. Its use of holonomy, phase closure, and transport is naturally compared with Wilson loops, Berry phase, and geometric quantization. The braid and linking language overlaps with topological soliton, Skyrmion, Hopfion, and braid/preon models of particle structure. The S^3 and SO(4) scale-rotation generator is adjacent to hyperspherical cosmology, closed FRW models, and Finsler-style indicatrix geometry. SAT differs from these prior approaches by treating these ingredients as parts of a single scale-recursive worldline geometry rather than as separate constructions.

That paragraph does three good things: it shows scholarship, prevents “this is just X” objections, and clarifies the novelty claim.

A few immediate cleanup notes from this batch:

The Blockwave constants are already close to standard effective-field-theory language. The file describes M\_P, \\mu^4, f^2, \\rho, \\alpha\_{1,2}/M\_^2, \\beta/M\_^2, \\lambda, \\kappa\_0, \\kappa\_2, and g\_h as controlling gravity, compact-phase stiffness, time-flow elasticity, nonlinear couplings, and filament stiffness [oai\_citation:0‡2025-10-23\_08:02\_THE GEOMETRY OF CONSTANTS

Going through the major physical constants as they appear in SAT:Blockwave setup, entirely verbally.

First, there is the Planck mass. This is capital M sub P. It sets the scale for gravitational interactio.txt](sediment://file\_0000000012b871f5864d5f0c86c5df75). This should be framed as an EFT-like action with a compact scalar, constrained timelike vector, and defect-current sector. That will make it much more legible.

The Q-number document is important because it corrects a major ambiguity: Q should not mean “particle identity” loosely. It is best defined as fundamental filament/strand count, with coiling order as a separate hierarchy variable. The document explicitly says He-3 is Q=9, second-order supercoil, while nucleons are Q=3, mesons Q=2, and photons/neutrinos are Q=1 single-coil excitations . That should become a hard glossary rule.

The H1/proton issue should be flagged as a sanity check. The Q-number notes correctly recognize that if hydrogen-1 were a second-order coil distinct from a proton, there should already be a large observed anomaly between atomic hydrogen and bare protons; absent that, the conservative formal rule should be: H1 nucleus = proton = first-order Q=3 braid, while deuterium/tritium are higher nuclear composites, not second-order versions of H1 unless a distinct observable is predicted .

The pulsar/phase-snap papers need terminology softening. They currently present v\_{crit}\\approx0.2387c, \\theta\_{obs}\\approx0.246 rad, Z\_3 closure, an Achromatic Phase Snap, and an 80% luminosity drop as exact consequences of lattice geometry . For outside readers, “Geometric Corner” should become critical projection angle, “Achromatic Phase Snap” should become holonomy-locked phase slip, and “Geometric Blackout” should become visible-sector projection suppression. Keep the SAT terms, but introduce the standard term first.

The Relativistic–Quantum Isomorphism is best positioned as a geometric–spectral correspondence: GR as the long-wavelength/geodesic curvature regime, QM as the short-wavelength/eigenmode regime on the same S^3 worldline geometry. The file explicitly maps GR to low-frequency global bending and QM to high-frequency local vibrational modes, with Laplace–Beltrami eigenvalues on S^3 giving discrete spectra . Prior art here includes spectral geometry, Kaluza–Klein mode spectra, geometric quantization, and loop/holonomy approaches.

The Whirligig should be treated as an analog model, not a primary ontology. The visual summary describes it as a “mechanical Fourier paper,” with a sphere nested inside a torus, input equations mapped to sphere coordinates, multi-axis rotation, and projection to Lissajous-like curves . The standard term is mechanical analog computer / epicyclic Fourier synthesizer / torus-flow visualization of coupled phase dynamics.

This batch gives you a much sharper formal vocabulary. The biggest hard rule I’d add now is:

Never use Q alone without also stating coiling order. Q = strand count. Order = hierarchy/coarse-graining level. Observable identity = function of Q, order, topology, and projection class.

Yes — that correction is important, and I agree with the framing.

The Whirligig and Universal Indicatrix should not be presented as ontology. They are better described as geometric solver tools or geometric translation engines: devices for converting algebraic, dynamical, or physical relationships into constrained geometric motion so that equivalence, deformation paths, invariants, and failure modes become easier to inspect.

That also fits your stated epistemic stance:

SAT has no final ontological commitment. Models are representational tools. Ontology is not directly accessible. The task is to find the model geometry that most economically preserves, translates, and predicts the observable structure.

So I would revise the dictionary entry like this:

REVISED DICTIONARY ENTRY

Universal Indicatrix (UI) SAT term: Universal Indicatrix

Formal category: geometric solver; scale-rotation trajectory generator; geometric translation engine; constraint-space parameterization; SO(4)-based solver manifold.

Function: A mathematical tool that maps equations, trajectories, or physical law-sectors into a shared geometric representation using scale r(λ) and rotation R(λ). It is used to compare theories, generate derivation paths, expose invariants, and identify minimal transformations between formulations.

Not ontology: The UI is not claimed to be what the universe "really is." It is a representation/solver for exploring structural equivalence.

Prior-art neighbors: geometric constraint solvers; dynamic geometry systems; symbolic geometry theorem provers; geometric algebra solvers; Hamiltonian/phase-space reformulations; variational/geodesic solvers; moving-frame methods; Lie-group integrators.

And:

Whirligig SAT term: Whirligig

Formal category: mechanical geometric solver; analog geometric computer; physical/visual model of constrained phase transport; torus-flow / Lissajous / epicyclic solver.

Function: A visualization and reasoning device that encodes inputs as rotations, phase couplings, and projections, producing interpretable curves or spectra. It helps reveal possible transforms between equation systems.

Not ontology: The Whirligig is not a literal claim about the universe. It is a mechanical/visual solver for exploring geometric structure.

Prior-art neighbors: harmonographs; epicyclic gear trains; analog computers; planimeters and integraphs; nomograms; dynamic geometry software; Fourier synthesizers; Lissajous figures; geometric constraint solvers.

The current research phrase “geometric solver” is a good umbrella, but it has several subfields. The most relevant prior-art clusters are:

1. Geometric constraint solving Solving configurations of points, lines, curves, distances, angles, incidences, and constraints.
2. Automated geometry theorem proving Symbolic or neuro-symbolic systems that prove geometric relations.
3. Dynamic geometry systems with deduction Interactive geometry environments coupled to algebraic/symbolic solvers.
4. Geometric algebra solvers Clifford/geometric algebra used as a compact computational language for geometry, transformations, and constraints.
5. Lie-group / moving-frame solvers Solving dynamics by encoding motion in SO(n), SE(n), SU(n), frame bundles, connections, and holonomies.
6. Variational geometric solvers Finding curves, geodesics, minimal surfaces, or action-minimizing paths in constrained spaces.
7. Analog geometric computation Mechanical devices that solve or represent equations through geometry: planimeters, harmonic analyzers, differential analyzers, integraphs, gear-train computers, and epicyclic mechanisms.

The recent literature is indeed increasingly active around AI and geometric solvers. Recent surveys discuss “geometry problem solving” as a broad area involving diagrams, symbolic tools, theorem proving, and multi-hop geometric reasoning; one 2025 survey is explicitly titled Towards Geometry Problem Solving in the Large Model Era and frames geometry problem solving as requiring multi-step diagrammatic and symbolic reasoning. Another 2025 survey covers deep learning for geometry problem solving, including geometric diagrams, semantic parsing, and theorem proving. There is also 2024–2026 work on dynamic geometry systems with integrated automated deduction and hybrid symbolic/deep-learning geometry-solving frameworks.

For SAT, the key phrasing should be:

The Universal Indicatrix and Whirligig are not ontological postulates. They are geometric solvers: representation engines for translating physical laws into constrained scale-rotation trajectories and comparing those trajectories through invariants, spectra, holonomy, and minimal deformation paths.

A concise paper-ready version:

We use the term Universal Indicatrix for a geometric solver rather than an ontological substrate. The UI maps equations or dynamical systems into a shared scale-rotation configuration space, allowing their invariants, holonomies, and minimal deformation paths to be compared. The Whirligig is the corresponding mechanical/visual analog: a torus-flow solver that renders coupled phase constraints as projected curves. Neither construction is assumed to be the literal structure of reality; both are representational tools for testing structural equivalence between models.

That sentence should probably go near the front of any paper that uses UI/Whirligig language. It will prevent a lot of wrong objections.

I’ve got the next batch. This one adds a more formal mathematical layer: 4DHH, Blockwave/action language, metric emergence, topological inversion, gravitational nanostructure, backbleed/light-cone language, and formal-proof/Lean-style rigor.

The biggest editorial alignment is this: several of these files already explicitly say SAT should avoid ontological assertions in publication and treat its objects as mathematical models or metaphors for abstract 4D calculations . That supports your point: UI/Whirligig/4DHH should be framed as geometric solver machinery, not as claims about what reality “really is.”
